```
authors/<author-email>.webp    avatar served to browsers (preferred); a .png/.jpg
                               sibling, if present, is the share-card source
authors/<author-email>.wav     voice-clone clip (build-only input, never served)
```

Discoverable by file listing, no central config to keep in sync, one author onboarding is a few file commits, **no env-var fallback** — the same reasoning (and the same `safeEmailComponent` path guard, shared from `voiceResolution.ts`) as the voice clip. `resolveAuthorProfile` returns a structured failure naming the gap; a post whose author has no resolvable profile is logged and simply gets **no byline** (degrade, don't fail the build). One folder per author keeps the runtime-boundary honest in a different way: only the `.json`/avatar are published (the avatar copied under a public handle, §below), while the `.wav` is read only by the offline build — so co-location doesn't blur "served" vs "build input".

**The load-bearing constraint: the served byline must never re-leak the email.** The whole reason `<meta name="author-email">` is [stripped from served HTML](#why-email-and-not-userid) is to keep the address away from crawlers. So the email is *only ever a disk/join key* — every value that reaches the client is derived from the public **`handle`** (a sanitized, lowercased slug from the explicit `handle`, else the X link's last segment, else a name-slug), never the email. The served avatar lives at `/assets/authors/<handle>.<ext>`, not `/.../<email>.<ext>`, and the public map the client fetches (`/assets/authors.json`, keyed by post path) carries no email at all. A build-time check confirms `dist/` contains no address.

**Handle derivation is ASCII-deterministic or it fails the build — never a silent guess.** The slug folds Latin accents with NFKD (`José`→`jose`, `café`→`cafe`), the same unambiguous fold the [heading slugger](#heading-deep-links) uses. But it deliberately **does not transliterate non-Latin scripts**: a Cyrillic, Greek, Arabic, Hebrew, CJK, or Korean name — or a non-decomposable Latin letter like `Ł` — has no single correct romanization (a kanji name has several readings; an off-the-shelf transliterator would pick the *Chinese* pinyin of a Japanese name), so guessing would silently mislabel the author and could collide two authors on `/assets/authors/author.<ext>`. Instead `resolveHandle` returns null for such a name, `resolveAuthorProfile` marks the failure **`fatal`**, and `buildAuthorMap` **throws** — so the build (and therefore CI) fails with an actionable message rather than shipping a wrong or colliding handle. The fix is the author's to make explicitly: set an ASCII `handle` in `authors/<email>.json` (a chosen romanization, e.g. `"tanaka"`) — the first-priority source, and the escape hatch for any name the deterministic fold can't handle. An explicit handle is authoritative: if it's itself non-ASCII, that's fatal too (we don't fall back to the name behind the author's back). Rejected alternative: an auto-transliteration library (`@sindresorhus/slugify`, `transliteration`) — it would *guess* a romanization, which is wrong-by-construction for multi-reading scripts and trades a loud, fixable error for a silent, hard-to-notice mislabel.

**The byline is rendered client-side** (`client/byline.ts`), a progressive-enhancement module like the player, comments, and figures — *not* injected into the HTML at build time. The reason is structural: a post is served as an **opaque static bundle in both dev and prod** — Bun's `HTMLBundle` in dev (which can't be rewritten the way the player/comments references are; see `createDevServer.ts`) and Cloudflare's `ASSETS` binding in prod. Only `dist/` gets the [post-build HTML rewrite](#build-time-html-strip), so a build-time *body* injection would appear in prod but not dev — exactly the drift the rest of the architecture avoids by making in-page features client modules. Client rendering is the one path identical in both. (Crawler/no-JS author metadata is not lost: it's emitted into `<head>` as JSON-LD/Open Graph at build time — see [Structured data](#structured-data-schemaorg-open-graph-twitter-card). The visible byline is for humans; the structured data is for machines.)

**One builder for dev and prod** (`buildAuthorMap`): the prod build (`copy-static.ts`) writes `dist/assets/authors.json` and publishes each avatar under its public handle; the dev server serves the identical map and avatars fresh per request from the same function — so a new post or edited profile shows on reload without a restart, and dev/prod bylines can't diverge.

**Avatar format: WebP for the page, PNG/JPEG for the share card.** Discovery prefers `.webp` (`AVATAR_EXTS`), the format served to the browser byline — a 144px WebP is ~2.5 KB against a 192px PNG's ~49 KB (Lighthouse's `image-delivery` insight; the byline `<img>` is also `loading="eager"`, being above the fold). The [share-card renderer](#share-cards) can't use it, though — satori/resvg decode only PNG/JPEG — so `share-card.ts`'s `avatarDataUri` falls back to a same-name `.png`/`.jpg` sibling for the card, and an author who wants their photo on social cards keeps one alongside the `.webp` (no sibling ⇒ the card renders name-only, degrade-don't-fail). The two consumers reading one resolved avatar is why the split lives at the format layer, not in two separate fields.

**Byline CSS lives in `client/base.css`, not `narrator.css`** — for the same reason the page-global layer was extracted there (see [Opting out of narration](#opting-out-of-narration)): the byline must appear on **every** post, including narration opt-out posts that never link `narrator.css`. The article root is found via the shared `[data-narration-src]` selector (present even on opt-out posts), and the block fills a build-time reserved placeholder so it never shifts the page on mount (falling back to a sibling insert after the lede / title when no reserve is present). See [Layout stability](#layout-stability-reserving-client-injected-chrome-cls).

**Social-link icons (FontAwesome Free v7 SVGs, inlined as text).** Each entry in the profile's `links` map (`{ x, github, bluesky, mastodon, … }`) renders as an icon-only anchor: the brand mark is inlined as `<svg fill="currentColor">` and `aria-label`/`title` carry the human label so it stays readable to screen readers and hover-discoverable to sighted users. We import the SVGs as text (`import faGithub from "@fortawesome/fontawesome-free/svgs/brands/github.svg" with { type: "text" }`) — so they end up in the JS bundle directly: no webfont, no extra `<link>`, no second-asset fetch, and nothing for the CSP's `font-src 'self'` to govern. `fill="currentColor"` means the icon inherits the anchor's text color and hover state without any new CSS plumbing. The chosen-vs-fallback split mirrors the `links` map itself: a known brand key resolves to `{ label, svg }` and renders the icon; an unknown key still renders the link, just with its key as plain text — adding a new brand is one SVG import + one map entry. (We considered the `fa-github` CSS-class approach with the FontAwesome webfont, but it would require both relaxing `font-src` for the woff2 and shipping a second stylesheet whose `@font-face url()` references Bun's bundler would have to rewrite; inline-SVG dodges both.)

**"Last updated" lives in a `.post-meta` strip directly under the `<h1>` title — separate from the byline.** The byline is *author identity* (avatar + name + social links); the meta strip is *article metadata* — currently `Last updated <date>`. The placement under the title (rather than under the lede or next to the byline) is the standard blog pattern — NYT/Guardian/Substack put the date here. The strip rides a `-24px` negative top margin to read as a subtitle line rather than a separate paragraph block. The calibration holds across every post because engine-layout (not any per-post sheet) owns the default `<h1>` margin — so the constant is paired with a shared baseline, not tuned to one specific post's title sizing. A post that genuinely wants a different gap can override `.post-meta { margin-top: <x> }` from [`@layer post`](#cascade-layer-architecture).

**Engine attribution (`buildEngineAttribution`).** Every post ends with a subtle `Built with presidocs` line linking to the engine's GitHub repo — appended in the same `client/byline.ts` boot pass as the follow-CTA, sharing its `[data-narration-src]` anchor so it appears on narration opt-out posts too. Independent of the author profile (no data dependency), so it always renders; muted `--page-fg-muted` styling and no card framing so it never competes visually with the follow-CTA above it. The URL is hardcoded — a downstream blog that wants the attribution gone overrides one CSS rule (`.engine-attribution { display: none }`); the engine doesn't add a config knob for it because hiding an `display: none`-able element is already the lowest-friction opt-out the platform offers, and a config flag would be one more dev/prod synchronization point for negligible gain.

## Cascade-layer architecture

Code: `client/base.css`, `client/narrator.css`, `client/comments.css`.

Two coexistence problems shape the engine's CSS architecture:

1. **Engine-owned UI lives inside the post's `<article>`.** The `.post-meta` strip, the `.byline`, the `.author-cta` follow-CTA, the `.engine-attribution` line, and the per-heading `.heading-link` icon are all engine-injected children of the article. That placement is load-bearing — the comment-anchor model, the JSON-LD `mainEntityOfPage` semantics, and the player's `[data-narration-src]` mount all assume the byline and its peers are part of the article entity. The cost is cascade exposure: a per-post stylesheet that scopes its rules to an article-level class shares an ancestor with these components, so a generic selector in the post sheet (an `a` rule, a typography reset) can reach into engine UI it never intended to touch.
2. **The engine's article-level defaults must apply to every post.** Article column width, heading rhythm, lede sizing, inline code — these used to live in `narrator.css`, which only loads on narration posts. Opt-out posts had to redefine the same defaults in their own per-post sheets, and the two sets drifted. "Shared across all posts" should be owned at the page-global layer, not duplicated per narration mode.

Both are solved by the same structural mechanism: **CSS [cascade layers](https://www.w3.org/TR/css-cascade-5/#layering) (`@layer`)** declare an explicit precedence between groups of rules, so each concern lives in its own layer and the cascade resolves them by *ordering*, not by what selectors the post sheet ends up writing. The layer order is declared **first** — the engine's bundler injects it as an inline `<style>` at the top of every layer-system page's `<head>`, ahead of any linked or JS-imported stylesheet (see *Pinning the order* below) — and restated in `client/base.css`:

```css
@layer engine-tokens, vendor, engine-layout, post, engine-components;
```

**These are flat, distinct top-level layer names — deliberately not dotted sublayers (`engine.tokens`, …).** A dotted name nests every `engine.*` under a single `engine` layer whose position is pinned at its *first* mention. Because `engine-tokens` is mentioned before `vendor`, that `engine` layer would sit before `vendor`, dragging `engine-components` (a sublayer) down *with* it — so the player and comments overrides would lose to Shikwasa's `@layer vendor` defaults. And the interleaving this order depends on — `vendor` between tokens and layout, `post` between layout and components — simply can't be expressed when those names share a parent. Dotted names would therefore demote the player and comments overrides below the vendor defaults in the pinned order (the player reverting to Shikwasa's default 120px chrome), and — because the order is pinned identically across builds (next paragraph) — would do so in every build, not just prod. Keep the names flat; don't "tidy" them into an `engine.*` namespace.

**Pinning the order: declared before any layer is created.** Cascade-layer precedence is fixed by the order in which each layer name is *first encountered while parsing*, and an `@layer a, b;` statement is **append-only** — it creates only names that don't yet exist, at the end of the current order, and can never reorder a name already created (there is no "always-first" layer feature in CSS). So the canonical statement is authoritative only if it is parsed before *any* stylesheet creates a layer. The two build paths don't agree on stylesheet order: the dev server serves each post's source HTML through Bun's bundler, and `client/narrator.ts` does `import "./shikwasa-vendor.css"` (which creates `@layer vendor`), so Bun's HMR can inject that — and the `engine-components` sheets — at module-eval time *ahead* of `base.css`, leaving `base.css`'s restatement only able to append the rest and inverting `engine-layout`/`engine-components`; the prod bundle instead concatenates `base.css` first.

The fix is to inject the order as an inline `<style>` that is the first CSS in the `<head>` — inline (not a `<link>`, which Bun's dev HMR re-injects via JS in module order, not document order — the reason `base.css`, linked first, still lost to Shikwasa; not JS-imported CSS, same problem) so it parses exactly where it sits, before any linked or imported sheet creates a layer. **Posts don't author it**; an engine bundler plugin injects it from a single source (`shared/cssLayers.ts`'s `CSS_LAYER_ORDER`), so a layer-order change is one edit, not a per-post sweep. The inject is **structural, not a string match**: `injectLayerOrderStyle` `prepend`s the `<style>` as the first `<head>` child via `HTMLRewriter` — the same engine every other head/body injector uses (`injectPwaHead`, `injectSiteFooter`, `injectPostChrome`). It fires only on the document's first real `<head>`, so it stays idempotent on unusual HTML and can't be fooled by a stray `<head` appearing earlier inside a comment, CDATA section, or attribute value — the failure modes the prior `/<head…>/` regex was open to. The font-preload inject (`injectFontPreloads`, prod-only) was flipped to the same `prepend` pattern; running after the layer-order inject, its preloads land just before the layer-order `<style>` (preloads create no layer, so cascade order is unaffected). The injection runs in both build paths through one plugin, `shared/bunHtmlHeadPlugin.ts` — which applies the cascade-layer order *and* the [site footer](#cascade-layer-architecture), combined because Bun `onLoad` is **first-match-wins**, so the engine's `.html` transforms must share a single plugin rather than registering two. It runs in **dev** via `bunfig.toml`'s `[serve.static].plugins`, which hooks Bun's *bundler* — where `loader: "html"` is accepted and the HTML's referenced scripts/styles are still discovered; a *runtime* `Bun.plugin()` can't, because its `onLoad` loader set excludes `html` (see `shared/bunFooterPlugin.ts`). In **prod** it's passed to `Bun.build`'s `plugins` in `generate/build-html.ts`. So dev and prod render identically — the footer included, no longer prod-only. Three guards keep it honest: `build-html.ts` asserts the built HTML declares the order before its bundled stylesheet; `createDevServer` throws at startup if the content repo's `bunfig.toml` doesn't register the plugin (so the dev half can't be silently dropped — engine-enforced, not a per-repo test); and `shared/cssLayers.test.ts` checks the injector and that `base.css`/the registry stay in sync. *Rejected:* authoring the statement per-post (couples the order *value* to every content file — an engine change would need editing each post); a runtime `Bun.plugin()` (rejects `loader: "html"`); and serving dev off the prod bundle (erases the divergence but discards HMR).

Five named layers, in cascade-precedence order (later wins):

- **`engine-tokens`** — `:root` design tokens (`--page-fg`, `--page-fg-muted`, `--page-bg`, and the `--font-sans` / `--font-mono` type families — see [Typography](#typography-self-hosted-web-fonts)) and the `box-sizing: border-box` reset. Lowest precedence so a post can rebrand a token from `@layer post` without `!important`.
- **`vendor`** — third-party stylesheets routed through this layer (today: the Shikwasa audio-player CSS, via `client/shikwasa-vendor.css`). Vendors ship their CSS unlayered, which would otherwise beat every layer — wrapping them in `vendor` demotes them to the lowest-precedence layer that touches their selectors, so the engine's overrides in `engine-components` win by ordering rather than by chasing the vendor's selector specificity. Inlined rather than `@import`ed because Bun's CSS bundler doesn't resolve bare npm specifiers from inside CSS — see `client/shikwasa-vendor.css` for the refresh-on-upgrade procedure.
- **`engine-layout`** — everything every post should look the same on: page-global `html`/`body` typography, `scroll-behavior`, the `.site-footer` block, the **article container** (`max-width: 768px` — matching the column width major reading apps converged on, top margin, side padding), the default heading rhythm (`article h1`/`h2`/`h3`), `.lede` sizing, inline `code` styling. Lives in `base.css`, not `narrator.css`, so it applies whether or not the post loads the player. Declared below `post` so a post that wants a wider column or a bigger title still wins from its own `@layer post`.
- **`post`** — every per-post stylesheet (`figures/*.css`) opts in by wrapping its rules in `@layer post { … }`. Article-body styling — titles, paragraphs, prose anchors, figures, tables, callouts — lives here. Stylesheets that don't opt in stay unlayered and keep today's behavior, so the migration is incremental: no opt-in is a regression, only a missed protection.
- **`engine-components`** — the five article-children components above, plus the rest of `narrator.css` (the floating player dock, chapter strip, `.narration-active` highlight, the player-reservation `padding-bottom`) and `comments.css` (the action bar, cards column, highlight spans). Declared last so it doesn't matter whether a post sheet writes `a`, `article a`, or `.<slug> a`: the engine rule wins by layer ordering, the byline icons stay `--page-fg-muted`, the follow-CTA stays neutral, the "Last updated" line stays in its calibrated slot, the heading-link affordance keeps its hover behavior, and the comments / narration UI render the same way across posts.

**The escape hatch is unlayered.** A post that *deliberately* wants to restyle an engine component writes an unlayered rule — no `@layer` wrapper — because **unlayered rules beat every layer**, regardless of specificity. So the override surface is preserved: a downstream blog that wants the engine attribution gone still writes `.engine-attribution { display: none }` (and `display: none` collapses the element across the cascade-layer fence regardless of property resolution); a blog that wants pink byline icons writes an unlayered `.byline-link { color: hotpink }`. The change is that *accidental* overrides — a post-scoped `a` rule reaching into the byline — no longer happen.

**Post-meta sits on a calibrated negative top margin, paired with a shared baseline.** Inside `engine-components`, `.post-meta { margin: -24px .25rem 0 }` pulls the strip up against the title so it reads as a subtitle line. The constant only works because `engine-layout` owns the default `<h1>` margin — every post inherits the same h1 rhythm by default, so the -24px is calibrated against a fixed baseline rather than a moving target. A post that overrides h1 margins from `@layer post` is making a deliberate departure and is expected to override the post-meta margin alongside it; the inverse problem (engine guessing what arbitrary h1 sizing each post might choose) is exactly what owning the default at the engine layer avoids.

**Why this over the alternatives.** Three approaches were considered and rejected. (1) **Move engine components outside `<article>`** — would fix the cascade leak but breaks the article-as-`mainEntityOfPage` JSON-LD invariant, breaks the comments-layer block walker assumptions, and doesn't address the meta-strip positioning bug (which is layout, not cascade). (2) **Shadow DOM per engine component** — bulletproof style isolation, but loses text selection across the boundary, loses the documented `display: none` opt-out, and is architecturally heavy for a five-component surface. (3) **`all: revert-layer` on engine-component roots** — works in principle but is a wrecking ball: resets `box-sizing`, `font-family`, every inherited property, every animation, requiring a long piecemeal re-application list that duplicates what `engine-components` already says. The layer ordering gets the same "engine wins" outcome without any of those costs.

**Authoring contract.** Per-post stylesheets opt into `@layer post`; the rule is spelled out in [`authoring/authoringRules.md`](./authoring/authoringRules.md). A future build-time lint that flags unlayered selectors matching engine-component classes (or unscoped `a`/`h1`/`p` rules inside the article) is **deferred** — the layer ordering prevents the bug, so the lint is at most a quality-of-life check on top, worth revisiting only after we see whether authors still trip on the surface.

## Typography (self-hosted web fonts)

The blog ships **two self-hosted web fonts** — Red Hat Text for prose, Red Hat Mono for code and data — declared once with `@font-face` in `base.css` and exposed as the `--font-sans` / `--font-mono` tokens. Every font declaration across the engine stylesheets *and* the content repo's `figures/*.css` resolves to those two vars, so the blog's type is a single edit; the only literals left are the inline-SVG static fallbacks in post markup, where a presentation attribute can't reference a custom property.

**Why a web font instead of a `system-ui` / `ui-monospace` stack.** A system stack resolves to a different face on every reader's OS — Segoe UI / Consolas on Windows, San Francisco / Menlo on macOS, DejaVu on Linux — each with different metrics, so the same line wraps to a different number of lines per reader. For body prose that's invisible; for the [animated figures](#animated-figures) it is not. A caption or readout that fits its reserved height on the author's font overflows on a wider one, the figure's box grows mid-animation, and every block below it shifts — and the author can't see it from their own machine. The divergence splits three surfaces that have to agree: the live page, the headless browser that [captures figures into the video](#video-export), and the headless browser that asserts figure height-invariance. One self-hosted face collapses all three onto identical wrapping — the only way a figure proven stable in CI is also stable for an arbitrary reader.

**Preloaded for swap-stability.** The faces are `font-display: swap`, so the system fallback paints first and Red Hat swaps in when it loads. If that swap lands *after* first paint it reflows the article: the fallback and Red Hat have different metrics, so text re-wraps and every block below shifts — a cumulative-layout-shift (CLS) cost that grows with how late the font arrives. To avoid it, the build **preloads the above-the-fold faces** (Red Hat Text 400, 500, 700 — the weights the first viewport renders, so their swap is the reflow `cls-culprits-insight` attributes to the article): `bunHtmlHeadPlugin.injectFontPreloads` prepends `<link rel="preload" as="font" type="font/woff2" crossorigin>` as the first `<head>` children, **prod-only** (dev inlines the faces as `data:` URIs, so a preload there is a wasted fetch). The remaining faces (600 / italic, the mono pair) stay unpreloaded — below the fold or rare, so preloading them would only contend with the critical bundle. The fetch then starts with the document and the swap lands at or before first paint — no shift. (A metric-matched fallback `@font-face` with `size-adjust`, tuned so even a late cold-load swap is zero-shift, would harden this further; not currently done.) A *preload `<link>`* changes load timing only, not rendered glyph metrics — so, unlike a change to the woff2 bytes, it needs no figure-capture cache bump (`figureCacheKey.ts`).

**Openly licensed, so we *can* self-host.** Self-hosting a font is redistribution, which a proprietary face (Segoe UI, San Francisco, Consolas) forbids outright — so the family had to be freely licensed, and that was a selection criterion, not an afterthought. Red Hat Text and Red Hat Mono are **SIL Open Font License 1.1**, which permits bundling and serving the files (its one restriction — don't sell the font on its own — we don't). The OFL requires the license to accompany every redistributed copy, and self-hosting the woff2 *is* redistribution: the woff2 name tables carry the copyright notice and a license URL but not the full license text, so `client/fonts/OFL.txt` is the stand-alone copy the OFL accepts, and `generate/copy-static.ts` ships it to `dist/fonts/` beside the woff2 (served at `/fonts/OFL.txt`) — the license travelling with the files, not a human-facing attribution link, is what the OFL asks for. It also keeps the blog's *type* as reusable as its content: a fork of the engine inherits a font it is allowed to ship, with no licensing asterisk to chase.

**Self-hosted, not a font CDN.** Same posture as the inline-SVG social icons: a third-party font request leaks the reader's visit and would force the CSP's `font-src` open past `'self'`. Serving the woff2 from our own origin keeps `font-src 'self'` intact and adds no third-party dependency. We ship a latin-subset woff2 per weight the prose and figures actually use (Text 400/500/600/700 + 400 italic, Mono 400/700), so nothing is browser-synthesized.

**Inline in dev, external in prod — forced by the CSP, not just performance.** `@font-face` uses a *relative* `url("./fonts/*.woff2")`. In **dev**, Bun's `serve.static` bundler inlines each as a base64 `data:` URI — fine there, because dev serves post HTML through Bun's static routes, which never pass through the [document CSP](#http-security-headers), and inlining keeps the figure capture and height gate font-faithful with nothing extra to serve. In **prod** that same inline would be *blocked*: `font-src 'self'` does not cover `data:`, so an inlined font never loads for a reader. So `generate/build-html.ts` passes `external: ["*.woff2"]` to `Bun.build`, which leaves the `url()` verbatim; `generate/copy-static.ts` copies the files to `dist/fonts/`; and the bundled CSS chunk, emitted at the dist root, resolves `./fonts/x.woff2` to the same-origin `/fonts/x.woff2` that `font-src 'self'` permits. The performance win rides along: Bun bundles each HTML entry's CSS into its own chunk and does *not* share chunks across entries, so an inlined font would duplicate ~100 KB into every post's CSS and re-download on each navigation — where the external file is fetched once and cached site-wide.

*Rejected:* an **absolute** `url("/fonts/…")` (or absolute `<link>`) — Bun's bundler tries to resolve it against the filesystem and errors, in both the dev bundler and `Bun.build`, so the relative form is the only one that survives both paths; and a **dev-side `/fonts/*` route** mirroring prod — dev already inlines, and a 404 there would silently fall the figures back to a system font and break the height gate without breaking the build.

**Landing and legal pages repeat the declaration.** They link `landing.css`, not `base.css`, so `landing.css` carries its own standalone copy of the `@font-face` block and the two tokens — same families, same files — rather than depending on a stylesheet it never loads.

## Layout stability: reserving client-injected chrome (CLS)

Code: `shared/articleChromeReserve.ts`.

The engine renders its in-article chrome **client-side** for [dev/prod parity](#author-profiles-and-bylines) — the [back-link](#back-to-all-posts), the [byline + "Last updated" strip](#author-profiles-and-bylines), the [copy-markdown](#copy-as-markdown) + [subscribe](#subscribe-controls) control row, and the [narration dock](#audio-player). Each mounts *after* the document parses (some after a `fetch`), so without intervention each one pushes the article below it down as it appears — a cumulative-layout-shift (CLS) cost. The fix is to **reserve each element's box at build time** so the client swap is layout-neutral; the [font swap](#typography-self-hosted-web-fonts) is the other half of the page's CLS and is handled separately by the preload.

**One injector, both environments (`shared/articleChromeReserve.ts` via `bunHtmlHeadPlugin`).** The reservations are emitted by the same [`bunHtmlHeadPlugin`](#cascade-layer-architecture) that injects the cascade-layer order and footer — so they run in **both** dev (the `[serve.static]` plugin) and prod (`build-html`'s `Bun.build`), and dev and prod stay identical. (The injection rides this plugin rather than the [served-HTML strip](#build-time-html-strip) because the strip is prod-only: reserving there would stabilize prod while leaving the dev server shifting.) The pass is post-scoped (only files under `posts/`) and idempotent.

**Two reservation shapes, matched to how each element mounts:**

- **Replace-in-place (back-link, byline, post-meta).** The build emits a fixed-height, empty `*-reserve` placeholder where the element will land (`.back-link-reserve`, `.byline-reserve`, `.post-meta-reserve`); `base.css` gives the placeholder the *same* box (margin + min-height) as the real element; the client `replaceWith`s it on mount. Identical boxes ⇒ the swap is a single zero-shift layout pass. One subtlety: `.back-link` is `display: flex; width: fit-content`, not `inline-flex` — as a block box its height matches the block placeholder exactly, whereas an `inline-flex` would sit in an anonymous line box whose strut (the article's line-height) makes it a few px taller than the reserve, reintroducing a shift.
- **Append-into (the control row).** Copy-markdown and subscribe both mount into the *same* slot, so a 1:1 replace doesn't fit. The build instead emits a min-height `.subctl-zone` flex container; `client/copyMarkdown.ts` appends into it, and `client/subscribe.ts`'s `copyMd.after(row)` then lands its row in that same zone — so subscribe needs no change. The zone holds its reserved one-row height as the controls fill it.

**The narration dock is reveal-after-mount, not reserve.** The [dock](#audio-player) is `position: fixed`, so it never reflows the article — but its empty box grew *upward* (it's bottom-anchored) as Shikwasa filled it, a self-shift attributed to `.narrate-dock`. Reserving a fixed element's height would just paint an empty player frame; instead the build ships the dock in its existing `[data-hidden="true"]` state (translated off-screen, `opacity: 0`) and `client/narrator.ts` reveals it (`revealDock()`) only once the player has mounted. The reveal is a `transform`/`opacity` transition — neither triggers layout-shift — so the dock costs zero CLS, with a deliberate "slides up when ready" entrance as a bonus. The error path reveals it too, so the "run `bun run generate`" nudge still shows. Shipping a JS-only feature (empty containers a script fills) hidden until JS runs is correct progressive enhancement.

**Data-gated reserves read the same source the client does.** The back-link and control zone are unconditional (they always render); the byline reserve is emitted only when the post has an author profile, and the post-meta reserve only when it has a version — gated on the **same** [`buildAuthorMap`](#author-profiles-and-bylines) / public-post-versions data the byline fetches at runtime (and which `createDevServer` serves), so the reserved space always matches what actually renders.

The behaviour is pinned by `client/byline.test.ts`, `client/backLink.test.ts`, `client/copyMarkdown.test.ts`, and `shared/articleChromeReserve.test.ts`. (A known residual: the *dev server only* shows a small early-layout settle of the first `<section>` — not chrome, fonts, or a JS mount, a pre-existing dev-rendering artifact, absent in prod — so it's not reader-facing.)

## Heading deep-links

Code: `client/headerLinks.ts`.

A long-form post is read in pieces — a reader (or the author, sharing context with someone) should be able to copy `…/posts/foo#problem-heading` to a section, not just the post. The minimum we want: an icon-only "copy link" affordance on every heading, discoverable on hover, that copies the absolute URL with brief feedback. Like the player, comments, byline, and figures, it's a progressive-enhancement client module — and lives in [`base.css`](#opting-out-of-narration), not a feature stylesheet, because every post needs it including [narration opt-out posts](#opting-out-of-narration) that never link `narrator.css`.

**A real `<a href="#id">`, not a `<button>`.** Plain click is the primary path, but right-click → "Copy link address" and Cmd/Ctrl-click → "Open in new tab" come along for free, and the heading stays semantically self-linked for screen readers and crawlers. The click handler then `preventDefault`s the native hash-scroll — the reader is already *looking* at the heading, so jolting the viewport on a "copy" gesture is the wrong default — copies the URL, and reflects the section in the address bar via `history.replaceState`. Replace, not push: repeated clicks shouldn't pile up history entries the user would have to press Back through. Modifier-key clicks (Cmd/Ctrl/Shift/Alt, and any non-primary button) early-return so the browser's native handling stays untouched.

**Author-supplied ids are preserved; missing ones are backfilled with slug-of-text.** A heading with no `id` gets `slugify(textContent)` (lowercased ASCII, hyphen-collapsed, deduped against every existing id in the document with a `-2`/`-3` suffix). Existing ids are left alone — this is load-bearing because the same heading ids anchor both [the `<mark name>` ↔ `id` pairing](#connecting-spoken-text-to-blog-content) the narration depends on and [comment threads keyed `id:<heading-id>`](#anchoring-the-web-annotation-target-model). Silently re-slugging an author-written id would orphan every anchor that points at it.

**Hash-stability for comments.** The comments layer hashes each block's `textContent` and flags a mismatch as [outdated](#stale-anchors-orphan--flag), so appending the link icon must not change `textContent`. The icon is built from FontAwesome SVGs whose markup contains only `<path>` (no text nodes — the FontAwesome attribution lives in a `<!-- -->` comment, which isn't part of `textContent`), wrapped in `<span>`/`<a>` elements that carry only `aria-label`. None of those tags are in the comments walker's [`BLOCK_TAGS`](#comments) set either, so the icon is invisible to the block walker too — no new commentable block, no hash drift on existing ones.

**Position and visibility — left gutter on hover, inline-after-text on touch.** On hover-capable devices the icon sits in the left gutter (`position: absolute; right: 100%`) — the GitHub/Docusaurus convention readers already know — and is hidden via `opacity: 0` until any of `h2/h3/h4:hover`, the icon's own `:hover`, or `:focus-visible` fires (so Tab-users can reach it via keyboard). On `@media (hover: none)` the gutter trick fails — a narrow mobile viewport hugs the left edge, so an absolute icon at `right: 100%` clips off-screen — so the touch fallback switches to `position: static` (inline after the heading text) and `opacity: 0.55` (always faintly visible, since there's no hover to trigger the reveal). Scope is `h2`/`h3`/`h4`: `h1` is the page title (the URL already points at it), `h5`/`h6` rare enough in long-form prose to skip until a need shows up. The same scope gains a mirror-image [`.heading-speaker` button](#two-level-chapters-parts--sub-chapters) on the *right*, but that one is injected by `narrator.ts` (it needs the manifest) and only on narrated posts — an independent enhancement that happens to flank the same heading.

**Scroll re-anchor on *load*, never on click.** When someone arrives with `#problem-heading` already in the URL, the browser's initial hash-scroll fires *before* progressive-enhancement modules (byline injection, this module's id-backfill) finish settling the DOM. The targeted heading may have moved by the time JS is done, so `reanchorToHashIfNeeded()` runs once at the end of `boot()` and re-`scrollIntoView`s the target. The click-copy path doesn't re-anchor (the heading hasn't moved) and explicitly suppresses the scroll — different cases, opposite handling. The `html { scroll-behavior: smooth }` rule (with a `prefers-reduced-motion` override) lives in `base.css` so any future in-page anchor (TOC, drawer link, anything) gets smooth-scroll for free without each feature wiring its own.

## Author-only block-id labels

Code: `client/figureCopyId.ts`.

Authoring leans on stable ids: a figure is staged by `<mark figure="…">`, a paragraph is referenced by id in a prompt to a coding assistant ("rewrite the paragraph `zswap-body`…"), and both are how a `<mark>` or a comment anchors. But the id lives in the source, not on the rendered page, so using one means digging back through the HTML. This module surfaces them: on the rendered post, every `<figure id>` and every `<p id>` in the article body gets a small monospace id label floating in the **left gutter** — the same `right: 100%` column the [heading deep-link](#heading-deep-links) uses, so the page's author affordances line up down one margin. Each label reads `#id` — an anchor-style cue that also lets a figure be found on the page with a `#id` find-in-page search — while clicking it copies the **bare** id (no `#`), flipping green with a trailing check, mirroring the narration drawer's per-segment id label. Like the player/comments/heading-link layers it's a progressive-enhancement module and lives in [`base.css`](#opting-out-of-narration), so it reaches [narration opt-out posts](#opting-out-of-narration) too.

**Author/localhost-only — inert for every reader and in prod.** The labels appear only when two gates pass: the host is `localhost`/`127.0.0.1`, and the server-authoritative `isAuthor` flag from `/post-version` is true — the same gate the narrator's author tools and [per-segment regeneration](#per-segment-regeneration-dev-author-only) use, and for the same reason (the source-only `<meta name="author-email">` is stripped in prod, so a DOM-based authorship check would read false there anyway). A non-localhost visitor short-circuits before any network call; either gate failing is a complete no-op — no labels, no DOM changes. Re-running the installer is idempotent (each block is tagged `data-figure-id-copy` / `data-paragraph-id-copy`).

**Figures carry the id as text; paragraphs must not — which also decides what find-in-page can match.** A `<figure>` is not a [commentable block](#comments), so its label holds the id in a real text node — which lets the author drag-select it for a plain Ctrl+C (`user-select: all` on the id) *and* makes it a find-in-page target, so a `#id` search jumps to the figure. The leading `#` is a separate, non-selectable span (`user-select: none`), so it shows and is findable as part of `#id`, yet neither a drag-select Ctrl+C nor the click handler (which copies the closure's bare `id`, not the rendered text) ever carries it. A `<p>` *is* a commentable block, and the comments layer hashes each block's `textContent` to anchor threads (the same [hash-stability hazard](#heading-deep-links) the heading deep-link tiptoes around). A text node here would leak the id into that hash and silently flag every thread on the paragraph as [outdated](#stale-anchors-orphan--flag). So the paragraph label is an empty `<button>` carrying the id in a `data-pid` attribute, rendered (with its `#`) through a CSS `::before { content: "#" attr(data-pid) }` — which `textContent` never sees, and `<button>` being phrasing content keeps it valid inside `<p>`. The cost of generated content is twofold and the same root cause: the paragraph id can't be drag-selected the way a figure id can, *and* find-in-page can't match it (it ignores `::before` content) — so the paragraph `#` is a visual cue only, not a Ctrl+F target. The click-to-copy path is identical for both.

## Citation deep-links

Code: `client/citationLink.ts`.

Heading deep-links above point at a *section*; the content is [CC-BY](#what-were-building), explicitly meant to be cited and reused, so a reader should also be able to point someone at a specific *sentence*. The [comments](#comments) layer already captures an exact passage (quote + ~32-char prefix/suffix) — but it is login-gated and produces a private CRDT annotation, not a shareable URL, so a logged-out reader (the common case, and the one Lighthouse measures) can't use it. `citationLink.ts` closes that gap: a small progressive-enhancement module — a sibling of `headerLinks.ts`, eager like it (a selection listener is cheap) and likewise living in `base.css` so [narration opt-out posts](#opting-out-of-narration) get it too — that turns any text selection in the article body into a [W3C Text Fragment][TextFragments] URL (`…/posts/foo#:~:text=…`).

**The consume side is free; only the *generation* ships.** Text-fragment *rendering* (scroll-to + highlight on arrival) is Baseline across modern engines, and a non-supporting browser simply lands at the page top — graceful degradation — so the reader who *opens* a citation link is shipped nothing. Only the *generator* runs, lazily, and only once the reader makes a selection worth citing.

**One selection bar, not two.** On a non-collapsed selection whose both ends sit in article prose, a small "Copy link" button copies the absolute citation URL with the same icon-swap + "Copied!" feedback the heading-link affordance uses. *Where* it appears depends on whether the [comments](#comments) layer is also offering its bar:

- **Logged-in desktop reader (commentable selection):** the comment action bar already appears *above* the selection with a "Comment" button. Rather than add a second, near-identical dark pill *below* (two competing controls — the reader can't tell which is which, and the one below can be the only one visible when the bar above clips off the top of the viewport), the "Copy link" button is a **sibling inside that one bar**: `[💬 Comment] [❝ Copy link]`. The comment layer owns rendering it (`comments.ts` calls `citationForRange`), and it tells the standalone button to **stand down** (`setCommentBarActive`) so there's exactly one bar.
- **Everyone else (logged-out, or a selection with no comment bar):** the standalone "Copy link" button appears just *below* the selection rect. This is the gap the comment path can't serve — a logged-out reader (the common case) still gets a citation link.

Either button is a real `<button>` (keyboard-focusable), and `mousedown` + `preventDefault` keeps the selection alive while it's pressed — the same trick the comment bar uses. Both bar buttons appear together the instant the bar does: the citation link is generated **on click**, not speculatively, so nothing runs on the (continuous) `selectionchange` path; the generator chunk is merely *pre-warmed* when the bar appears so the on-click copy is instant. (The standalone button, with no sibling to stay in sync with, instead debounces generation on selection-settle and only shows once a link is ready.) The gesture is **scoped to body prose**: selections inside a `<figure>` or inline `<svg>` are ignored (`:~:text=` can't reliably anchor into graphics), and the comment column (mounted on `<body>`) is excluded for free because it sits outside the article. The audio player's drawer is *also* outside the article, but it is **not** excluded for free, and this is a trap worth naming: a selection's container node can't simply be handed to `article.contains(...)`, because Firefox fires `selectionchange` with the anchor in the player's **native-anonymous content** (the media-control internals / the seek-bar thumb), whose nodes live in a different security principal — reading *any* property of one (even `.nodeType`) or passing it to `.contains()` throws (`Permission denied to access property "nodeType"` / `Argument 1 does not implement interface Node`). So both selection handlers — the citation path here and the comment layer's `captureSelection` — first run the shared `isInspectableSelectionNode` guard (`document.contains(node)` behind a try/catch), treating a throw, a detached node, or null as "not ours" before touching the node.

**Generation: the spec algorithm, via `fragment-generation-utils`, lazily.** Emitting a *correct* directive is the whole problem, and it is harder than it looks because the consumer is the **browser**, not our code — the [Text Fragment matcher][TextFragments] (mirrored at [`./specs/ScrollToTextFragment-spec.html`](./specs/ScrollToTextFragment-spec.html)) imposes two rules a naive string builder can't honour, and getting either wrong yields a link that silently scrolls nowhere — the worst failure for a *citation* feature:

- **Word boundaries.** The matcher only accepts a fragment that begins *and* ends on a Unicode (UAX&nbsp;#29) word boundary — `text=range` matches "mountain range" but not "color orange" or "forest ranger". So a selection that starts or ends mid-word (a plain mouse-drag, not a double-click) must be **expanded to word boundaries** before it's emitted, or it matches nothing.
- **Uniqueness.** A `#:~:text=` directive has **no metadata slot** — no content hash, no block id — and the element-id part (`#heading:~:text=…`) does *not* scope the search (the matcher always searches the whole document; the id is only a fallback scroll target). So the *only* way to pin a repeated phrase is to **iteratively extend** prefix/suffix and the `start,end` range until the match is provably unique. (This is why the [comments layer's](#comments) content-hash anchoring does **not** port here: that hash is resolved by *our* JS; the browser ignores it.)

Both are exactly what GoogleChromeLabs' **`fragment-generation-utils`** (Apache-2.0, zero runtime deps, the generator half of `text-fragments-polyfill`) implements, tracking Chrome's shipping behaviour. We feed it the live `Selection`; it returns a unique, word-bounded fragment or a `status` of `AMBIGUOUS`/`TIMEOUT` when it can't. We **gate on that status: a successful fragment becomes the precise passage link; otherwise we degrade to the nearest section's `#id` link** (relabelling the button "Copy section link") rather than emit a broken one. The generator is **lazy-imported behind the gesture** — its own chunk (Bun `splitting: true`, the same deferral the narrator/comments use), `import()`ed once when the reader first makes a citable selection — so it costs **zero at first paint**. (An earlier cut shipped a home-grown emitter that did one-shot, one-word disambiguation and no boundary-snapping; it failed in both ways above on real long-form posts, which is what triggered adopting the library — the upgrade the original proposal pre-authorised at this one call site.)

The DOM-free surface — directive encoding (`directiveFromFragment` / `encodeTextFragmentTerm`), URL composition (`buildCitationHref`), and the degrade decision (`chooseCitation`) — is unit-tested without a DOM, and `installCitationLink` (idempotent mount of a hidden, keyboard-reachable button) under the happy-dom harness. The *generation* itself can't run there (it needs real layout + `Intl.Segmenter` + a live `Selection`), so the two behaviours that motivated the library — a **mid-word selection still producing a link that resolves**, and a **repeated phrase being disambiguated** — are proven in the real-browser tier (`e2e/citationLink.e2e.ts`), which selects mid-word, copies the link, reopens it, and asserts the page scrolled to the passage. A second real-browser test (`e2e/citationCommentBar.e2e.ts`) covers the merge: a logged-in reader gets one bar with both "Comment" and "Copy link", and the standalone button stays suppressed.

## Testing layout

Tests are colocated with the code they cover — `<module>.test.ts` sits next to `<module>.ts` rather than in a separate `tests/` tree, matching how `types/` and `proposals/` are absent in favor of "the code owns its own surface." `bun test` is the single entry point; the `presidocs/package.json` `test` script runs nothing more elaborate than that.

The suite splits into three layers by what each test needs from the runtime:

- **Server / generate / shared** — the bulk of the tests, exercising offline pipeline code (`generate/`), the Worker entry points (`server/`), and runtime-agnostic helpers (`shared/`). These run in plain Bun: no DOM, no preload, no fetch stubs at the file boundary. Anything that runs only on the offline build or the edge belongs here.
- **Client (DOM) — per-file opt-in happy-dom** — see [DOM testing harness](#dom-testing-harness) below.
- **Tier-0 pure extractions** — math/string functions lifted *out* of DOM-coupled client modules into `shared/` so they can be tested without happy-dom. `shared/narratorTiming.ts` (mark + word bisect) is the canonical example; `client/commentsStale.ts` (segment-hash drift check), `client/narratorDom.ts` (hash parsing, capture-controls localStorage, keyboard-shortcut guard, top-level chapter resolver), and `client/commentsDom.ts` (block walker, hash-stability normalization, hide-all storage) follow the same pattern. The rule: anything testable as `(input) → output` lives outside the class, gets a dedicated `.test.ts`, and runs without any DOM. The DOM-coupled wrappers in `narrator.ts` / `comments.ts` then call into these helpers, so a regression in the math is caught at the pure-test layer before it can reach a DOM test.

### happy-dom or e2e? (the decision rubric)

happy-dom is a JS reimplementation of the DOM, not a browser: it parses markup, matches selectors, dispatches events, and resolves *plain* computed style — but it has no layout engine, no rendering, no a11y tree, no audio decoder, and an incomplete CSS model. The split below follows directly from that. **Default to happy-dom; escalate to e2e only when the thing under test is something happy-dom provably cannot model.** When in doubt, the fast check is: write the one-line probe and read what `getComputedStyle`/`getBoundingClientRect`/`getByRole` actually returns under happy-dom — if it returns empty/zero/nothing for the property you're asserting, that's your answer.

**happy-dom is enough when the assertion is structural or logical:**

- **DOM construction** — element/attribute/text shape after a render or enhancement (byline placement, anchor injection, drawer markup).
- **State-machine / pure logic** — resolvers, parsers, guards, slugify/dedupe, localStorage round-trips.
- **Event wiring** — click/keydown handlers fire and mutate state (focus moves, classes toggle, storage writes).
- **Selector matching + *unlayered* specificity** — `getComputedStyle` does resolve which plain rule wins by specificity, so a non-`@layer` style conflict is assertable here.

**Reach for e2e (real browser, `e2e/*.e2e.ts`) when correctness lives in something happy-dom doesn't implement:**

- **Cascade layers (`@layer`)** — happy-dom ignores `@layer` entirely: `getComputedStyle` returns `""` for *any* property set inside a `@layer {}` block (verified — an unlayered conflict resolves, the identical layered conflict returns empty). So any bug whose mechanism is layer *ordering* beating specificity is invisible to happy-dom and a DOM test would pass regardless of the bug. Example: the drawer's spoken-segment `<article>` inheriting base.css's `engine-layout` `article { margin: 64px }` over its own `engine-components` rule — the only honest guard is real-browser `getComputedStyle(segment).marginTop`.
- **Real CSS layout / geometry** — anything reading `getBoundingClientRect()` (dock clearance, bottom spacer), CSS anchor positioning, `position: sticky`, `:popover-open` placement, light-dismiss. happy-dom returns zeros unless you hand-mock each rect, which only tests the mock.
- **Accessibility tree** — landmark role/name (`complementary` named "Comments", never `feed`), `aria-pressed`, `posinset`/`setsize`, the article/`<details>` snapshot. happy-dom builds no a11y tree, so `getByRole`-style assertions need a real engine.
- **End-to-end stateful flows with validation** — flows where the assertion depends on the whole pipeline running for real: the comment selection → compose → submit → CRDT → anchoring → upload path, asserting cards track highlights, drafts don't scroll-to-top, below-fold cards persist, overlaps cascade. The reusable session-minting + UI-driven seeding "comment fixture" lives here.
- **Real audio playback** — the rAF tick, `timeupdate`, active-mark application, Shikwasa chapter-strip render. happy-dom's `<audio>` has no decoder, so `play()` never advances time. (Today this is manual via `scripts/release-check.md`; e2e would be its natural home.)
- **Touch / device emulation** — a coarse pointer, no hover, a mobile UA, a device-width viewport: `@media (pointer: coarse)` / `(hover: none)` rules, tap-only flows, and mobile-only UI (the single comments button + menu, the tap-to-popover comment cards). happy-dom has no media/device model, and a *narrow viewport in a desktop e2e context still reports a fine pointer with hover* — so genuine-touch behaviour needs real device emulation, the **mobile tier** (`e2e/mobile.e2e.ts`, harness `MOBILE_DEVICE` / `newMobileContext`). The viewport-gated *logic* (the menu flow, selection capture/retention) doesn't, and runs faster in a plain narrow context — `e2e/commentsMenuFlow.e2e.ts`.
- **Substrate the browser owns** — Service Worker lifecycle, push delivery, OAuth redirects, OS clipboard. e2e (with a `dev:edge` target for the SW) or manual; never happy-dom.

The cost asymmetry behind the default: a happy-dom test runs Chrome-free in the unit pass; an e2e test boots system Chrome + a dev server and runs on-demand (`bun run test:e2e`). So a bug that *can* be pinned at the pure or DOM layer should be — escalate only when the rubric above says happy-dom literally cannot see the failure.

### DOM testing harness

Code: co-located with `client/*.ts`.

Client modules target the browser, not the Bun runtime, so testing them needs `document` / `window` / `localStorage`-as-Storage. We use **happy-dom**, registered per-file rather than via a global Bun preload.

**Registration is per-file via `import "../happydom.ts";`**. The helper at `presidocs/happydom.ts` calls `GlobalRegistrator.register()` exactly once (a `globalThis.__HAPPY_DOM_REGISTERED__` flag short-circuits subsequent imports). Each DOM test file's first import is `"../happydom.ts"`, before any import of the module under test — happy-dom must install `document` before the module's top-level code runs.

**Why per-file, not the docs-recommended global `[test] preload`.** The happy-dom docs recommend a `bunfig.toml` entry like `preload = ["./happydom.ts"]` that registers the browser environment for *every* test file. Tried, rejected. Two concrete breakages in the existing server / generate suite:

1. **`commentsStore.test.ts`'s `localStorage` shim** — the file installs an in-memory `localStorage` for testing the CRDT store outside a browser. happy-dom registers `globalThis.localStorage` as a *non-writable* property (`Object.defineProperty` with `writable: false`), so a plain `globalThis.localStorage = shim` assignment throws once happy-dom has run. Defensible fix: rewrite the shim install as `Object.defineProperty(globalThis, "localStorage", { value: shim, writable: true, configurable: true })`. That's now the project pattern for ANY test that wants to replace a happy-dom-installed global (the same shape is used in `client/postVersion.test.ts`'s `fetch` stub).
2. **`audio-pipeline.test.ts`'s `Bun.spawn` calls** — happy-dom mutates fields on `process` (its own browser shim for `process.stdio` triplet semantics differs from Bun's), and `Bun.spawn`'s stdio config rejects the mutated shape with `TypeError: stdio must be an array of 'inherit', 'ignore', or null`. There's no clean shim for this on the test side; the fix is to keep happy-dom out of those tests entirely, which per-file opt-in does for free.

Per-file opt-in keeps happy-dom's blast radius scoped to exactly the files that ask for it. The docs' advice still applies for projects that are 100% browser-side; in this mixed engine, per-file is the right tradeoff.

**One `beforeEach` reset.** Every DOM test starts with `beforeEach(() => { document.body.innerHTML = ""; })`. Without it, fixture markup from one test pollutes the next within the same file. Tests that also touch `localStorage` follow with `localStorage.clear()`, and tests that replace globals (the fetch stub pattern) restore them in `afterEach` via `Object.defineProperty`.

**What's tested in this layer.** Pure-DOM construction and pure-state-machine logic in the four DOM-coupled client modules:

- `client/narratorDom.ts` — chapter-number resolver, divider-speaker "first below" element, capture-controls localStorage round-trip, keyboard-shortcut focus / modifier guard, deep-link hash parser. The full Narrator class isn't tested directly — its constructor needs Shikwasa Player, which needs real `<audio>` decoding (see "deliberately not tested" below).
- `client/commentsDom.ts` — block walker, hash-stability `normalizeText`, highlight-mute (`loadHighlightsHidden`/`saveHighlightsHidden`) localStorage. The full CommentSystem class isn't tested directly — it needs Automerge boot, identity fetch, polling controllers, and a CRDT store, all of which the same-file `commentsStore.test.ts` already exercises at the data layer without a DOM.
- `client/byline.ts` — placement rules (under `#lede` if present, else under `#title`, else prepended), profile rendering, the privacy property that the served avatar URL never embeds the author email, post-meta date formatting.
- `client/headerLinks.ts` — slugify rules + dedupe, idempotent id backfill, anchor injection.
- `client/citationLink.ts` — the pure Text Fragment emitter (verbatim vs `start,end` range, prefix/suffix disambiguation only when the quote isn't unique, hyphen-safe term encoding, clean citation-URL composition) plus the idempotent button mount.

**What's deliberately NOT tested in this layer.**

- **Anything that needs real audio playback** — the rAF tick reading `player.currentTime`, the active-mark application path (`updateActive` → `setActive`), Shikwasa's chapter-strip rendering, OS lock-screen widgets. happy-dom has a JS-visible `<audio>` element but no decoder, so `play()` doesn't advance time and `timeupdate` doesn't fire. The pure math (`computeActiveMark`, `findActiveWord` in `shared/narratorTiming.ts`) is covered server-style; the integration is covered by the manual release-check at `scripts/release-check.md`.
- **Real CSS layout** — the comment column's CSS-anchor-positioned cards (placement is engine work; happy-dom doesn't implement [CSS Anchor Positioning][CSSAnchorPos1]), `updateBottomSpacer()`'s use of `getBoundingClientRect()` to size the dock clearance, the desktop comment column's pinned identity card, the dim-on-load → reveal-on-scroll threshold for the sign-in CTA, the mobile popover's `:popover-open` placement + light-dismiss. happy-dom returns zeros for `getBoundingClientRect()` unless a test hand-mocks each element's rect, which produces tests that pass only against their own fake. The integration is real-browser territory; the manual checklist covers it.
- **Service Worker installation, push delivery, OAuth redirects, OS clipboard** — substrate behaviour the browser owns. No automated test in any tier here can verify these meaningfully; manual is the only honest layer.
- **Tier-2 modules where the bug-cost hasn't yet justified the test** — `identity.ts`, `commentsSync.ts`, `commentsAggregator.ts`, `resolutionsStore.ts`, `commentsPolling.ts`, `commentsApi.ts`, `resolutionsApi.ts`. Each is a small fetch wrapper or single-flight controller; the contract surface is exercised end-to-end by the integration of the modules that consume them. The rule is "first concrete regression motivates the test, not speculation."
- **Per-engine browser-quirk matrices.** We don't assert Chrome-vs-Firefox-vs-Safari *behaviour differences* as contracts — known per-engine quirks (auth flows, media-session, reader-view extraction) are accepted constraints, not bugs to chase. Chromium is the primary automated target; Safari/Firefox stay manual-quarterly (`scripts/release-check.md`). A cross-engine matrix would multiply flake for divergences a single-author blog can verify by hand.
- **Live-DOM reader extraction.** The [Markdown twin](#copy-as-markdown) runs Readability at build time, where it's deterministic and golden-tested; a browser's own reader view runs the same engine on the live post-JS DOM, a surface no test asserts. The served HTML is authored so that DOM extracts cleanly (the comment column is a real `<aside role="complementary">` Readability drops, the article carries `role="main"`), but the live-DOM result is best-effort — a Chromium e2e injecting Readability into the live page and golden-comparing is the piece worth automating, deferred until a live-DOM regression is actually observed.
- **The Cloudflare deploy surface itself** — the Worker running against *real* R2 / Static Assets / Range / cache-rule behaviour. The dev server already closes the runtime-parity gap with `getPlatformProxy()` (Miniflare-backed bindings) and `prodAudioSmoke.ts` covers the Range contract; the remaining edge behaviour (real bucket, real cache rules) is verified by the manual `dev:edge` smoke before deploy, not an automated tier.

**Real-browser harness (live).** A Playwright-driven e2e tier lives in `presidocs/e2e/`, driven by the `playwright` *library* (not `@playwright/test`) from inside `bun test` — one runner, two surfaces. Files are named `*.e2e.ts` so the default `bun test` glob (which matches `.test.`/`.spec.`) never touches them; they run on demand via `bun run test:e2e`, which keeps the unit run Chrome-free. `e2e/harness.ts` launches the **system Chrome** (no ~150 MB binary download) and boots a content-repo dev server on an ephemeral port (the engine has no posts of its own; which repo it drives, next paragraph). What's covered today: the cheapest-highest-value real-browser checks — real CSS layout (a heading's `getBoundingClientRect()` is non-zero, which happy-dom can't produce) and an accessibility-tree guard (the comments column resolves as a `complementary` landmark named "Comments" and is never a `feed`, unassertable in happy-dom because it builds no a11y tree). Also live: **comment-card positioning** (`e2e/commentPositioning.e2e.ts`, Chromium + Firefox + WebKit) — it mints a dev session, drives the real selection → comment → submit flow (real CRDT + anchoring + upload), and asserts cards track their highlights, drafts anchor while composing (no scroll-to-top), below-fold cards stay put (don't vanish with their anchor), overlapping cards cascade instead of stacking, and the header rail stays pinned in the gutter. The session-minting + UI-driven seeding it introduced is the reusable "comment fixture." (The mobile popover's below-the-highlight placement used to be asserted here too, faking mobile by resizing this desktop context; it now runs on a real emulated device in the mobile tier — see below.) Also live: **per-post accessibility** (`e2e/axe.e2e.ts`) — runs axe-core over the landing page and every post (the rendered WCAG A/AA bars a static pass can't see — contrast, JS-generated figure ARIA, Label-in-Name), excluding the vendored Shikwasa player; `color-contrast` is a hard gate (with a fixed per-node exemption roster; see [`DESIGN.md`](./DESIGN.md) §2) and it still ratchets the w=0 Label-in-Name rule. Non-text figure contrast is the separate `e2e/figureContrast.e2e.ts` gate. Also live: the comment-card **accessibility-tree snapshot** (`e2e/commentA11ySnapshot.e2e.ts`) — each seeded card resolves as an `article` with no `aria-posinset`/`aria-setsize` inside the Comments `complementary` landmark (never a `feed`), the per-card guard the column-level landmark test complements (proposal 22 §2; the version-history `<details>` expanded/collapsed snapshot stays deferred behind a multi-version fixture). Also live: the **mobile tier** (`e2e/mobile.e2e.ts`) — Playwright **device emulation** (harness `MOBILE_DEVICE` = Pixel 5 → `isMobile` + `hasTouch` + mobile UA, a genuine coarse pointer with no hover, which a bare narrow viewport can't fake) so the blog's mobile-only surfaces run as a phone drives them: it asserts the emulated environment is real (`pointer: coarse`, `hover: none`, `maxTouchPoints > 0`, device-width layout) and that the comments button is mobile-only (absent from the a11y tree at desktop width, exposed below 1100px) and opens the one menu on a real touch **tap** (`aria-expanded` flips, `.cmt-menu` reaches `:popover-open`). It checks the menu's two identity states: **signed in**, the menu carries "Signed in as X" + Sign out and there is **no** standing `.cmt-identity` pill (its computed `display` is `none`); **signed out**, the menu offers the sign-in providers and — even with a selection held — shows **no** redundant "Leave comment on selection" entry. It covers **tap-to-popover placement** on the real device: seed a comment, reload to a pristine reader state, then a real `tap()` on the highlight opens its card as a top-layer popover (`:popover-open`) anchored **under the button** (`position-anchor: --cmt-comments-btn`, `position-area` resolving rather than dropped to `none`, the card sitting below the button). A companion test drives the full **interaction** as a state machine: tap a highlight to open, re-tap to toggle closed, tap a *different* highlight to swap — discriminating the open card by its `data-thread-id` (every card now shares the button's anchor, so anchor identity can't tell them apart) so "swap" proves a different card surfaced and only one is ever live. A further test guards the **anti-stacking** invariant — two comments on the *same passage* never stack on mobile (`adjustCardStacking` early-returns under `isMobile`, the layout is one-popover-at-a-time) — and platform **light-dismiss** (a tap below the open card closes it). And it drives the **highlight mute** from its new home — a *menu item*, not the old one-tap FAB — flattening highlights and persisting the choice across a reload (the inverse label shows on reopen). Seeding a comment on mobile drives the real menu path (select → tap button → "Leave comment on selection"), not the desktop action bar, which is suppressed below 1100px. The logic-heavy half of that flow — which doesn't need genuine touch — is split into a faster **narrow-viewport tier** (`e2e/commentsMenuFlow.e2e.ts`): because mobile mode is purely viewport-gated and a real `.click()` still fires `pointerdown`, it runs in a plain sub-1100px desktop context (mouse) and asserts the headline **select → menu → compose** flow with **retention correctness** — after a reload the persisted thread's quote matches the originally-selected text, proving the captured range survived the collapsing button tap — plus the pending-selection cue (`body.cmt-has-selection`) tracking the live selection, the no-selection hint state, and **figure → thread in a card under the button**. (The `--cmt-bottom-sheet` overflow fallback — used when a long thread can't fit below the button — still awaits a short-viewport fixture to force the overflow.) (Emulation gotchas for any future mobile test. **Small `position: fixed` targets** — the 44px comments button — need `{ force: true }`: Playwright's actionability hit-test mis-resolves the tap point under a >1 `deviceScaleFactor`, reporting `<html> intercepts pointer events`; assert `visible` + a11y-tree presence first, then force the tap at its centre. **Inline highlight spans** need force *and* a deliberate scroll: a low highlight can land under the fixed narration dock (a plain `tap()` then reports the `<p>`/dock "intercepts pointer events"), and because the engine sets `html { scroll-behavior: smooth }`, a force-tap fired right after a plain `scrollIntoView` lands at the pre-scroll coordinate and is silently lost — so scroll the span to centre *instantly* (`behavior: "instant"`), let it settle, then force-tap. A real 44px touch target / a real text run is genuinely tappable; the desktop *click* path confirms hittability.) It also carries **media emulation** on the device context. *Reduced motion* is honored and asserted: under `reducedMotion: "reduce"` base.css drops `html { scroll-behavior: smooth }` to `auto` site-wide and the comment card's `transition: top 180ms` is cancelled to `none` (0s) — proven to track the preference by a contrast against a plain mobile context where scrolling stays `smooth`. *Colour scheme* is asserted as the engine's deliberate **light-only** invariant rather than a dark theme it doesn't ship: under `colorScheme: "dark"` the OS preference reads dark (`matchMedia('(prefers-color-scheme: dark)')` true) yet the document declares no `color-scheme` (computed `normal`) and never flips — the article is light-only by design and the dock/player are forced dark unconditionally (not gated on `prefers-color-scheme`). Also live (and the highest-value tier — the reason the harness exists; the PWA + SW foundation has shipped, [Offline / PWA](#offline--pwa)): the **Service-Worker / PWA tier** (`e2e/serviceWorker.ts`). The SW deliberately doesn't register under the fast `bun run dev` server (`swRegister.ts` gates on `__BUN_DEV__`, and dev actively tears down any SW), so this tier drives the **built** worker under `wrangler dev` — the `dev:edge` target — via the harness's `startWranglerServer()` (`bun run build` + `wrangler dev` on an ephemeral port; `PRESIDOCS_E2E_SKIP_BUILD=1` reuses a fresh `dist/`). Because it's a heavy build+wrangler lane, it is named `serviceWorker.ts` *without* the `.e2e.ts` infix — exactly like `prodAudioSmoke.ts` — so `bun run test:e2e` skips it; it runs via its own `bun run test:e2e:sw`. It asserts: the SW reaches active and **controls** the page (`navigator.serviceWorker.controller` non-null); the **app shell precaches** at install (`/`, `/manifest.webmanifest`, `/assets/automerge.wasm` land in the deploy-versioned `static-<VERSION>` cache); the **online → offline** flow — a post visited online is re-served from cache after `context.setOffline(true)`, i.e. the network-first navigation handler falling back to `runtime-<VERSION>` (the core offline-reading guarantee); the **cache-first** branch for content-addressed assets — a hash-named JS/CSS chunk the page loads is asserted to land in the SW cache on the online visit and then resolve from cache while offline, so the page doesn't just *render* offline but *runs* (the offline app shell, distinct from the network-first HTML path); and the **cross-deploy cache reap** — a redeploy is simulated by overwriting the served `dist/sw.js` with a bumped `VERSION` (wrangler serves it fresh — `/sw.js` is `Cache-Control: no-cache`) and calling `registration.update()`; the byte-different script installs, `skipWaiting()` activates it, and its `activate` reap is asserted to delete the previous deploy's `static-<v1>`/`runtime-<v1>` caches while precaching the new `static-<v2>` (the mutated `dist/sw.js` is gitignored and snapshot-restored). It also verifies the **Web App Manifest / install shell** (the other half of "is this a PWA" — the runtime half is the SW above): a built, wrangler-served page advertises the manifest (`<link rel="manifest">` → `/manifest.webmanifest`) plus a `theme-color` and an `apple-touch-icon` (the iOS home-screen icon the manifest's own `icons` array doesn't cover), and the served `/manifest.webmanifest` is fetchable and **installable-shaped** — `name`, root `start_url`/`scope`/`id`, `display: standalone`, and 192px + 512px icons. This is a dev:edge-only concern because the head injection is a post-build rewrite (`shared/injectPwaHead.ts`), so the fast `bun run dev` source-HTML pages carry none of it; the injector's pure logic is unit-covered in `shared/injectPwaHead.test.ts`. One SW behavior stays deferred to the manual `dev:edge` smoke (DevTools → Application → Service Workers): the SW's own `cacheFirstRanged` **206** path (the prod Worker's Range contract is already covered by `prodAudioSmoke.ts`; the SW's branch awaits an audio fixture). Posture: Chromium-only; Safari/Firefox stay manual-quarterly via `scripts/release-check.md`. A CI lane exists (`.github/workflows/ci.yml`, two jobs against the self-contained fixture — see the fixture paragraph below): `test` runs typecheck + the unit suite + the Chromium `test:e2e` loop; `worker-tiers` runs this SW tier plus the CSP and broken-links gates (csp/links reuse the SW tier's build via `PRESIDOCS_E2E_SKIP_BUILD`). Also live (same heavy wrangler tier, own script `bun run test:e2e:csp`): the **prod-CSP console/violation gate** (`e2e/cspConsole.ts`) — the Best-Practices analogue of what the axe tier is to Accessibility. The *enforced* document CSP only exists on the built worker (`withSecurityHeaders` wraps the `HTMLBundle` routes the Bun dev server serves bare), so it drives the page under `wrangler dev`, registers a `securitypolicyviolation` listener before navigation, and over the landing + every post **fails on any CSP violation** while the figures/comments/Zod paths are exercised (scroll + registry wait). Non-CSP console noise (anonymous-user 401s, a missing favicon) is logged, not failed. It catches prod-CSP violations a static Lighthouse pass can miss — notably an interaction-only Zod 4 JIT-probe `eval` (Zod compiles validators with `new Function`, which `script-src` blocks; `z.config({ jitless: true })` in `shared/zodJitless.ts` opts out so Zod runs the interpreter and no violation fires) — and it guards the engine's own layer-order inline `<style>`, which `style-src` allows by hash (see [HTTP security headers](#http-security-headers)). Also live (same heavy wrangler tier, own script `bun run test:e2e:links`): the **broken-link / dead-fragment gate** (`e2e/brokenLinks.ts`) — there is otherwise *no* link or anchor validation in the engine (`audit-posts.ts` checks title/lang/meta/landmark/img-alt, nothing href-shaped), so a renamed slug, a moved asset, or a renamed `<mark>`-keyed heading id silently ships a 404. It runs [linkinator](https://www.npmjs.com/package/linkinator) (MIT, build-host-only `devDependency`, zero client/Worker bytes) recursively over the built site with `checkFragments`, failing on any internal `<a href>` that 404s or any `#fragment` that points at an id absent from the target page. **It crawls the running worker, not the `dist/` directory, on purpose:** the site's internal links are extensionless (`/posts/<slug>`, `/help`) and resolve only through Workers `html_handling` — a static-directory crawl 404s every one of them, and the obvious fix (rewriting extensionless→`.html` with linkinator's `urlRewriteExpressions`) makes pages reachable but silently *defeats* fragment checking (verified). Crawling the real worker (the same `startWranglerServer()` = `bun run build` + `wrangler dev` the SW/CSP tiers use) resolves extensionless URLs natively, so fragments are validated against the actual served HTML; `cleanUrls: true` matches the worker's clean-URL model so a post's relative asset refs (`../chunk-*`) resolve to the root like a real browser, not one directory too deep. The post's [Markdown twins](#copy-as-markdown) (`dist/posts/<slug>.md`) were considered as a no-server target and rejected: the HTML→Markdown transform strips every element `id` (Markdown has no place for `<h2 id="problem-heading">`), so the authored `#fragment` targets vanish while the links to them survive verbatim — a Markdown anchor-checker resolves those fragments against text-derived heading slugs (`#the-throughput-wall`) that don't match the authored ids, and would flag every live deep-link as broken. The Markdown is a lossy downstream export; validating it answers "is the `.md` twin internally consistent," not "does the shipped HTML have dead links" — a different artifact and a different question, and not even a conservative one (it manufactures false positives on exactly the authored-id layer this gate exists to check). External links are skipped in this publish-blocking lane (checking them would make the gate non-deterministic and leak third-party requests from the build host). Coverage boundary: it validates the **author-written** id/href layer (`#problem-heading`, `#oq1-what`, intra-post anchors, the `<mark>`↔heading wiring) and inter-page/asset links — all present in served HTML — but **not** the heading slugs `client/headerLinks.ts` backfills at runtime (absent from server-rendered markup; closing that would require emitting heading ids at build time). Like the SW/CSP tiers it's named without `.e2e.ts` so `bun run test:e2e` skips the build+wrangler weight; it runs via `bun run test:e2e:links`.

**Which content repo e2e drives (the self-contained fixture).** `e2e/harness.ts:resolveBlogDir()` defaults to the engine's own `templates/content-repo`, made bootable on first use by `ensureFixtureBlog()`: the `engine` → `../..` symlink, `bun link` + `bun install` (the template's normal one-time setup, automated), and a generated `SESSION_SECRET` written to both `.env` and `.dev.vars` (the dev server reads the first, the harness's session-minting the second — they must agree). Every product lands inside the template directory and is gitignored there; `bun run e2e:fixture` front-loads the slow first install (the CI entry point). The engine's e2e suite is therefore runnable from a bare engine checkout — no private content repo. `PRESIDOCS_E2E_BLOG` overrides the target, and each content repo carries its own `test:e2e*` scripts (`PRESIDOCS_E2E_BLOG=$PWD bun run --cwd engine test:e2e`), so the author's one-command flow still drives the real blog. The tests are content-agnostic to make that dual-target posture work: single-post tiers drive `firstPostSlug()` (alphabetically-first deployable post), and the figure gates drive `figurePostSlugs()` — every post whose *source markup* loads a `../figures/*.ts` module, detected from the markup rather than the runtime registry so a bundling/registration regression on a figure-bearing post fails the gate loudly instead of vacuously skipping it. The few content-anchored tests (the `lifecycle-figure` stepped-driving pair, the merge-figure coverage assertion) gate on their test-bed post being present in the driven repo. The fixture's `hello.html` is deliberately a real post — author-email meta, several long commentable paragraphs spanning >1000px (the positioning gate asserts real anchor geometry), a contract-conformant FigureJourney figure (`figures/heartbeat.ts`), and the full engine client-script set including `swRegister.ts` (the template also ships a `manifest.webmanifest` + placeholder `icons/`, without which the SW's app-shell `cache.addAll` would 404 and the install would fail) — while staying narration-free on disk (no generated audio), so the suite carries no TTS/MOSS dependency: audio-gated tests (the subscribe tier's podcast half) skip on it, and the build emits no `podcast.xml` (nor, since the autodiscovery link is gated on the same signal, any link to one). The heavy wrangler tiers run against the fixture too — that's what CI's `worker-tiers` job does.
